**Limits and questions not reached:** No general collective-bargaining entitlement, overtime exemption or presumption of individual consent is created. The Court does not decide a practice adopted after April 15, 1986 or employees with a representative legally capable of making the collective agreement.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm on both cohorts: the regular April 15, 1986 practice supplies the earlier-hire agreement; actual individual agreements remain available for later hires lacking a legally capable representative.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

No Supreme Court precedent is overruled, limited, or extended beyond its own terms; the Court decides only the two routes actually presented on this record.

### ERISA equitable relief against nonfiduciaries

#### Mertens v. Hewitt Associates, 508 U.S. 248 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1671.
**Decided:** June 1, 1993.

##### The reviewed compensatory award is not appropriate equitable relief

**Holding:** Section502(a)(3)’s appropriate equitable relief encompasses traditionally equitable categories of relief, not compensatory damages merely because a court of equity could award them in trust litigation. The requested monetary compensation from this admitted nonfiduciary for the plan’s losses is therefore unavailable under that provision; the Court does not bar every monetary equitable remedy or decide the antecedent, expressly disclaimed cause-of-action question.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court; Blackmun, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (5 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The expressly disclaimed antecedent cause of action is reserved, not rejected as jurisdictional. Other equitable monetary remedies are not categorically barred; restitution, unappealed claims and the separate malpractice disposition remain untouched.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm rejection of the reviewed compensatory claim under §502(a)(3); no liability finding or award.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Curtis v. Loether, 415 U.S.189: traditional legal compensation distinction applied.
- Chauffeurs, Teamsters & Helpers v. Terry, 494 U.S.558: remedy classification applied; trust forum alone does not decide the statutory category.
- Firestone Tire & Rubber Co. v. Bruch, 489 U.S.101: trust-law guidance preserved subject to express statutory limits.
- Clews v. Jamieson, 182 U.S.461: equity’s monetary powers acknowledged but distinguished from the statutory category.
- Massachusetts Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Russell, 473 U.S.134: enforcement-structure caution applied; its reserved question is now resolved only within this holding.
- United States v. Burke, May26,1992: its fractured judgment supplies no controlling general remedial-range test.
- Franklin v. Gwinnett County Public Schools, February26,1992: appropriate-remedy presumption distinguished because Congress expressly limited this provision to equitable relief.

### Federal supervision of employee benefit trusts

#### Local 144 Nursing Home Pension Fund v. Demisay, 508 U.S. 581 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-610.
**Decided:** June 14, 1993.

##### Section 302 restrains prohibited transactions

**Holding:** Section302(e) authorizes restraint of violations of the payment, demand, receipt and related prohibitions in §302(a) and (b); §302(c)(5) identifies qualifying trusts rather than a general supervisory remedy for later administration. Retaining these reserves supplies no transfer remedy without an actual prohibited transaction; payment to a nonqualifying trust remains subject to the statute.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court, Section 302 restrains prohibited transactions portion; Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The three-Justice pooled-fund rationale is noncontrolling. Separate ERISA asset-transfer and fiduciary claims remain unresolved; lawful receipt does not immunize later fiduciary misconduct.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the §302 transfer direction and remand; the court of appeals may consider the unreviewed ERISA claims previously rejected below.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Arroyo, 359 U.S.419 — lawful receipt distinguished from later diversion; contrary supervisory language is dictum.
- Amax, 453 U.S.322 — fiduciary independence retained; supervisory language does not establish jurisdiction.
- Robinson, 455 U.S.562 — reasonableness-review rejection retained; reserved supervisory scope resolved.
- Mertens, June 1, 1993 — unchanged; its remedy limit does not resolve these unreviewed ERISA claims.

### Employment discrimination and proof of pretext

#### St. Mary's Honor Center v. Hicks, 509 U.S. 502 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-602.
**Decided:** June 25, 1993.

##### Pretext alone does not command judgment, and circumstantial evidence need not be supplemented by a special category of proof

**Holding:** After an employer produces admissible evidence of nondiscriminatory reasons, a proven prima-facie case and rejection of the offered explanations do not automatically command judgment for the employee. Those circumstances may support a finding of intentional discrimination without a mandatory additional category of direct or independent proof; the Eighth Circuit’s automatic-liability mandate is vacated, while no majority here adopts a replacement persuasion allocation.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas (5 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Burdine’s persuasion allocation is not replaced. No universal Title VII/§1983 equivalence, clear-error decision, resolution of retaliation, remaining defenses, relief or applicability of the 1991 Act is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate the appellate automatic-liability mandate under Title VII and the related §1983 claim against Long and remand. No district-court vacatur, new burden allocation or judgment for either side is ordered. Existing district findings remain subject to proper review; retaliation remains for consideration and the unappealed §1981 dismissal is unchanged.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- McDonnell Douglas Corp. v. Green,411 U.S.792: indirect framework retained; no automatic liability holding adopted.
- Texas Department of Community Affairs v. Burdine,450 U.S.248: production stage retained; no five-Justice replacement of its persuasion rule.
- Postal Service Board of Governors v. Aikens,460 U.S.711: actual discrimination and circumstantial evidence remain central.
- Hazen, April20,1993: prohibited motive remains distinct from general unfairness; no added evidence category follows.

## Holdings volume: federal-taxation

# SUPREME COURT OF THE UNITED STATES: HOLDINGS REGISTER

**Last completed October Term:** 1992  
**Processed through:** July 26, 1993, after DeBoer v. DeBoer, No. A-64.  
**Edition:** September 17, 2026

## Federal Taxation

### Bankruptcy fiduciaries’ filing and payment duties

#### Holywell Corp. v. Smith, 503 U.S. 47 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 90-1361 and 90-1484.
**Decided:** February 25, 1992.
**Companion or consolidated matters:** *United States v. Smith*, No. 90-1484, consolidated with *Holywell*; both challenge the declaration of no filing or payment duty.

##### Whether the assignee of substantially all corporate property must file corporate returns and pay the associated tax

**Holding:** An assignee holding all or substantially all of a corporation’s property must make its income-tax return under §6012(b)(3), whether or not the corporation dissolves or the assignee continues its business. Smith’s receipt of the corporate estate property satisfies that category, and §6151(a) requires payment of the tax due with those returns.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; unanimous statutory construction.

**Material application:** The plan assigned substantially all corporate property to Smith for liquidation and distribution. The filing duty follows the statutory assignee category and does not depend on operating the business or dissolving the corporations.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No alternative statutory classification, exact income, deduction, penalty or tax amount is decided. The duties concern returns and accompanying payment after appointment, not taxes already due before appointment.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand in both dockets for Smith to file the prescribed corporate returns as assignee and pay the tax due with them under §6151; amounts and implementation are left to ordinary tax and bankruptcy procedures.

##### Whether Smith must file and pay as fiduciary of the separate liquidating trust holding Gould’s former estate property

**Holding:** Smith must file the separate liquidating trust’s return under §6012(b)(4) and pay the associated tax under §6151: the plan vested Gould’s estate property directly in a separate trust organized to liquidate and distribute the transferred assets and authorized Smith to perform those functions, making him its fiduciary despite limits on his discretion. Because the property did not first revest in Gould for contribution to the trust, the asserted grantor-trust theory does not displace Smith’s duty.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; unanimous trust-specific construction and application.

**Material application:** The plan created a separate trust to liquidate and distribute transferred assets within Treasury Regulation §301.7701-4(d); Smith’s powers satisfy §7701(a)(6) and Regulation §301.7701-6 despite limits on his discretion. The estate transferred Gould’s property directly to the trust; Gould did not first recover and contribute it.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No universal tax treatment for all liquidating trusts is adopted. Grantor provisions retain their operation when their predicates exist; the result of a different revesting-and-contribution arrangement is not decided. The Court computes no tax amount.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand in both dockets for Smith to file as trust fiduciary and pay the associated tax, with computation and implementation left to ordinary procedures.

##### Whether confirmation of a plan silent about taxes and the United States’ failure to object excuse later statutory duties

**Holding:** Confirmation under §1141(a) of a plan silent about taxes, coupled with the United States’ failure to object, does not excuse the statutory return and payment obligations imposed on this assignee and trust fiduciary after appointment. The binding treatment of properly encompassed preconfirmation claims does not establish an exemption from these later obligations.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; unanimous rejection of the confirmation defense.

**Material application:** The government seeks recognition of Smith’s postappointment duties, not payment from him of taxes already due before appointment. The plan neither stated nor adjudicated an exemption; §6151 connects payment to the required return’s due date.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court assumes, without deciding more broadly, that confirmation can bind creditors concerning properly encompassed preconfirmation claims. Preappointment taxes and express adjudication of existing tax claims remain outside the disposition.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The no-duty declaration is reversed in both dockets; confirmation supplies no defense to implementation of the postappointment filing and payment obligations.

### Capital expenditures

#### INDOPCO, Inc. v. Commissioner, 503 U.S. 79 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1278.
**Decided:** February 26, 1992.

##### Whether a separate asset is necessary for capitalization and whether these acquisition expenses are currently deductible

**Holding:** Creation or enhancement of a separate and distinct asset is a sufficient ground for capitalization under §263, not an indispensable condition; the character, duration and extent of a transaction’s benefits also matter. The professional expenses facilitating this acquisition’s established enduring corporate restructuring benefits are capital expenditures, not immediately deductible §162(a) operating expenses; mere incidental future benefit alone does not compel capitalization.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; unanimous statutory holding and application.

**Material application:** The investment-banking, legal and related expenses facilitated a negotiated acquisition producing established corporate benefits beyond the acquisition year: access to Unilever’s resources, simplified ownership and conversion from independent public corporation to subsidiary. Those benefits do not rest solely on a shareholder’s estate-planning preference or speculative profit estimates.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No automatic capitalization rule for every future benefit, acquisition or professional fee is adopted. Hostile acquisitions, failed transactions, unrelated fees and later cost-recovery periods are not decided; the ordinary taxpayer burden to establish a deduction remains unchanged.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm denial of the disputed immediate deductions and preserve capital classification. No new amortization schedule or additional tax assessment is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Commissioner v. Lincoln Savings & Loan Association*, 403 U.S. 345, is clarified: creating a separate asset is sufficient but not necessary for capitalization. *Welch v. Helvering*, 290 U.S. 111, supplies the contextual distinction between necessary business outlays and current expenses. *Woodward v. Commissioner*, 397 U.S. 572, and *United States v. Hilton Hotels Corp.*, 397 U.S. 580, connect professional costs to the capital acquisition they facilitate. *Commissioner v. Idaho Power Co.*, 418 U.S. 1, preserves capitalization despite an otherwise available expense category. *Interstate Transit Lines v. Commissioner*, 319 U.S. 590, leaves the taxpayer’s deduction burden unchanged.

### Income exclusions

#### United States v. Burke, 504 U.S. 229 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-42.
**Decided:** May 26, 1992.

##### Whether the identified pre-1991 Title VII settlement payments are excluded from gross income under §104(a)(2)

**Authority of the judgment:** The Sixth Circuit’s exclusion ruling is reversed, 6–3: White, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter support reversal; Stone, O’Connor and Thomas oppose it. The identified payments may not be excluded in the refund action. No controlling statutory rationale exists under *Marks*. Blackmun, White, Stevens and Kennedy rely on the old Title VII remedial scheme; Scalia separately construes the statute around physical or mental health; Souter accepts the regulation, recognizes competing tort and contract analogies, rejects making intangible remedies indispensable, and relies on narrow construction of uncertain exclusions. Neither concurrence is established as a logical subset of the plurality’s remedial classification.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The judgment creates no general remedial-range test, health-injury requirement or discrimination-recovery exclusion. No separate FICA wages issue, punitive-damages classification, ordinary wage settlement, distinct tort allocation, amended statutory regime or Title VII jury-trial question is decided. The settlement establishes no adjudicated discrimination finding. No independently sufficient alternative holding has five votes. Further proceedings must give effect to reversal, without enlarging the recovery or valuation; no tax amount, separate withholding theory or reopening of the settlement is ordered.

### Intergovernmental tax immunity

#### Barker v. Kansas, 503 U.S. 594 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-611.
**Decided:** April 21, 1992.

##### Whether Kansas may tax military retirement while exempting comparable state and local retirement benefits

**Holding:** For 4 U.S.C. §111, the military retirement benefits at issue are deferred compensation for past service, comparable to exempt Kansas state and local retirement benefits. Continued military status, restrictions and possible recall do not justify taxing the entire military retirement stream while exempting those comparable benefits; the asserted difference must directly relate to and justify the unequal tax treatment.

**Proposition-level authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas; nine votes control. Stevens and Thomas also concur separately without limiting their joins.

**Material application:** Rank and completed years of service principally determine these payments. Continued military status, discipline, employment restrictions and possible recall do not explain taxing the entire pension stream while exempting comparable state pensions.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Compensation specifically tied to new service, other military-status consequences, materially different tax classifications and preserved retroactivity or refund issues remain undecided. Other constitutional and state-law claims supply no independent holding; McKesson and Beam are not modified.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Kansas Supreme Court judgment is reversed and remanded for compliance with §111 and consideration of preserved retrospective-relief questions under then-governing law. No refund amount is awarded.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Davis v. Michigan Department of Treasury, 489 U.S. 803 — applied: retirement income receives §111 protection and a tax-relevant significant difference must justify unequal treatment.
- Phillips Chemical Co. v. Dumas Independent School District, 361 U.S. 376 — applied through Davis: the difference must directly relate to and justify the disparity.
- United States v. Tyler, 105 U.S. 244 — confined to its statutory pay-increase context; continuing military status does not establish current compensation for all purposes.
- McCarty v. McCarty, 453 U.S. 210 — distinguished: it reserved the characterization question and its community-property prohibition was displaced by the Uniformed Services Former Spouses’ Protection Act; no surviving rule authorizes this tax disparity.

### National Firearms Act making tax

#### United States v. Thompson/Center Arms Co., 504 U.S. 505 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-164.
**Decided:** June 8, 1992.

##### Whether the genuine unassembled dual-use Contender package incurs the short-barreled-rifle making tax

**Holding:** The National Firearms Act does not clearly treat the segregated Contender pistol and conversion package, which has genuine lawful pistol and long-rifle uses and has not been assembled as a short-barreled rifle, as making that regulated firearm. Because the same definition carries criminal consequences, the remaining ambiguity is resolved for the manufacturer in this civil refund action as well.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's plurality opinion joined by O'Connor, Scalia's concurrence joined by Thomas, and Stone's concurrence expressly share this case-specific construction and uniform residual lenity. These five form an actual majority at this level of generality; no complete opinion controls and no synthetic *Marks* rationale combines their different accounts of the ambiguity.

**Current force:** Remains controlling for the identified genuine dual-use package and the shared residual-lenity proposition.

**Material application:** Section 5845's rifle definition addresses design, making and intended shoulder firing, while other firearm definitions expressly address combinations of parts. The language and related definitions leave ambiguity for this package's lawful pistol and long-rifle configurations alongside its regulated short-rifle capability.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No complete opinion controls the broader division between permissible and regulated combinations. Actual short-rifle assembly, pure regulated-firearm kits, sham lawful configurations, miscellaneous or incomplete parts, unrelated firearm categories and separate criminal offenses remain unadjudicated. No general intent-to-assemble element, new criminal proof burden, storage protocol or exemption for all parts kits is created. Souter's broader utility taxonomy, Scalia and Thomas's separate shoulder-firing account and Stevens's civil-notice exception remain noncontrolling.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the Federal Circuit's refund judgment, 5–4, with Stone, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter and Thomas supporting affirmance and White, Blackmun, Stevens and Kennedy opposing. The $200 making tax is not owed on the segregated unassembled unit; no broader injunction or new remand command issues.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Crandon v. United States*, 494 U.S. 152, supplies one construction of criminally consequential language in civil litigation without changing criminal proof. *United States v. R. L. C.*, March 24, 1992, preserves its adult-Guidelines ceiling without establishing a shared new lenity method or firearms rule. *Marks v. United States*, 430 U.S. 188, supplies no synthesized broader rationale where the exact case-specific proposition already has five express supporters.

##### Whether making a firearm invariably requires final mechanical assembly

**Holding:** The National Firearms Act's references to manufacturing and otherwise producing a firearm are not confined to the final mechanical act of putting its parts together. The Federal Circuit's universal actual-assembly prerequisite is rejected without deciding tax liability for an unpresented pure-parts or sham arrangement.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter and O'Connor expressly adopt the construction; White's dissent, joined by Blackmun, Stevens and Kennedy, adopts the same predicate; and Stone's concurrence expressly rejects a universal final-assembly requirement while reserving pure-parts and sham liability. These seven directly support the exact proposition across writings. Scalia and Thomas disagree. This is an actual majority proposition, not a *Marks* synthesis or a join in the plurality's broader taxonomy.

**Current force:** Remains controlling as stated, without a categorical rule for the reserved arrangements.

**Material application:** Section 5845(i) separately identifies manufacturing, otherwise producing and putting together. Requiring final assembly for every form of making would deprive the broader production language of work. Production must still meet the enacted definition; regulatory purpose alone does not impose a tax.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No holding classifies every miscellaneous collection, incomplete kit or genuine alternative-use set as a firearm. Liability for pure-parts kits, sham configurations, actual short-rifle assembly and separate offenses remains outside the decision.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The narrow refund judgment remains affirmed; the Federal Circuit's universal actual-assembly rationale does not survive as the governing construction.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *United States v. Drasen*, 845 F.2d 731, supports the manufacturing construction without adjudicating its kits or adopting its broader notice and criminal-responsibility discussion. *Thompson/Center Arms Co. v. United States*, 924 F.2d 1041, is affirmed in judgment but its universal actual-assembly rationale is rejected.

### Assessment limitations for pass-through income

#### Bufferd v. Commissioner of Internal Revenue, 506 U.S. 523 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-7804.

**Decided:** January 25, 1993.

##### Which return starts limitations on the shareholder’s individual assessment

**Holding:** For an assessment of an S corporation shareholder’s individual income tax, the return that starts the ordinary § 6501(a) limitations period is the shareholder’s return, including applicable extensions and statutory exceptions. Expiration of the period associated with the corporation’s § 6037 information return does not bar correcting pass-through items in a timely shareholder assessment; the corporate return remains operative for corporate-level taxes to which it applies.

**Proposition-level authority:** White’s opinion for the Court, joined in full by the other eight Justices. All nine Justices adopt this rule and its explanation; it controls by a direct majority.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Automobile Club of Michigan v. Commissioner, 353 U.S. 180, 188 (1957), applied by analogy: a filing insufficient to compute the relevant taxpayer’s liability does not trigger that taxpayer’s assessment period; the holding is not a rule that informational completeness alone transforms one person’s return into another’s.
- Germantown Trust Co. v. Commissioner, 309 U.S. 304 (1940), distinguished in scope: a sufficient return for the same taxpayer may trigger limitations despite its form; it supplies no corporate-return substitute for a shareholder’s own return.
- Holywell Corp. v. Smith, February 25, 1992, left undisturbed: distinct fiduciary and trust filing duties do not decide the limitations question here.
No Supreme Court precedent is overruled. The contrary corporate-period rule applied in Kelley v. Commissioner, 877 F.2d 756 (9th Cir. 1989), cannot govern this shareholder-assessment question. No broader C corporation rule is decided.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The ordinary three-year period remains subject to the shareholder’s applicable extensions and statutory exceptions. The corporate return retains its corporate-tax function. Special audit regimes, the unpressed wording objection to the individual extension and any general C corporation rule are not decided; later unified-audit rules do not govern the 1979 liability.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the Second Circuit: the timely shareholder assessment is not barred by expiration of the corporate-return period.

### Depreciation of acquired intangible assets

#### Newark Morning Ledger Co. v. United States, 507 U.S. 546 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1135.

**Decided:** April 20, 1993.

##### Proof controls depreciation of the acquired customer intangible

**Holding:** Under section 167 and Regulation §1.167(a)-3, a taxpayer that proves an acquired intangible’s ascertainable value and limited useful life estimable with reasonable accuracy may depreciate it despite its resemblance to expected continued patronage. The taxpayer bears the substantial proof burden; unsupported assertions, indefinite or self-regenerating value and double allocation do not qualify, and goodwill remains nondepreciable.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, I; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Massey Motors, 364 U.S. 92 — applied: depreciation matches capital cost with useful contribution.
- Houston Chronicle, 481 F.2d 1240; Miller, 537 F.2d 446; Donrey, 809 F.2d 534 — persuasive proof-based reasoning adopted, not a categorical customer-list exemption.
- INDOPCO, February 26, 1992 — distinguished: capitalization and later cost recovery are separate questions.
- Red Wing Malting, 15 F.2d 626 — preserved for nonwasting goodwill; not extended to defeat proof of a distinct wasting asset.

##### The established subscriber findings satisfy the rule

**Holding:** The finite acquired subscriber cohort here satisfies the separate-value and reasonably measurable limited-life requirements on the existing trial findings. The useful-life and straight-line stipulation becomes operative only after those prerequisites are met; reversing the categorical goodwill rule neither supplies an unconditional concession nor requires proof of the entire case anew.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, II; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Houston Chronicle, 481 F.2d 1240 — applied through its evidentiary requirements; actual findings matter.
- Golden State Towel, 373 F.2d 938 — distinguished: the acquired cohort is found not to be a self-regenerating mass.
- Massey Motors, 364 U.S. 92 — applied to the proved finite-life asset; no universal valuation method adopted.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The finite purchased subscriber cohort excludes later automatic replacement customers; indefinite or self-regenerating goodwill remains nondepreciable. The useful-life and straight-line stipulation is conditional on the proved prerequisites. No universal valuation method, customer-list exemption or duplicate basis allocation is authorized.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the categorical goodwill judgment and remand consistently with the existing separate-value and finite-life findings and conditional stipulation. No later amortization regime or new tax amount is imposed.

### Home-office deductions

#### Commissioner v. Soliman, 506 U.S. 168 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-998.

**Decided:** January 12, 1993.

##### Comparative principal-place-of-business inquiry under former §280A(c)(1)(A)

**Holding:** Under then-effective §280A(c)(1)(A), identifying the principal place of business requires comparing the relative importance of activities performed at each business location and the time spent there; necessary administration or the lack of another office alone does not make a home office principal. Customer contact or service delivery receives great but nonconclusive weight, special facilities may add weight, time is particularly useful when the functions do not settle the comparison, and a home office does not qualify by default when no place emerges as principal.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O’Connor and Souter: six votes for this formulation and its application. Thomas and Scalia agree only with the judgment on their separately stated approach; Stevens dissents. The Court opinion controls without a fractured-decision inquiry.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Malat v. Riddell, 383 U.S. 569 (1966), and Crane v. Commissioner, 331 U.S. 1 (1947): applied for ordinary meaning in revenue statutes; neither is displaced.
- Welch v. Helvering, 290 U.S. 111 (1933): retained for the fact-sensitive character of deduction questions; it does not prevent articulating consistent statutory considerations.
- The Fourth Circuit’s necessity/substantial-time/no-other-office test is rejected because it fails to compare locations; no earlier Supreme Court principal-place test is overruled.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Exclusive and regular business use remain prerequisites. Patient or client visits are not indispensable under subsection (A); subsection (B)’s normal-course meetings and subsection (C)’s unattached structures remain separate unadjudicated alternatives. The employer-convenience condition applies to employee use, not this self-employed taxpayer. No fixed arithmetic weighting or default home-office qualification is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the Fourth Circuit and disallow the 1983 principal-place home-office deduction on the hospital-service and time facts. Other deductions, including unappealed travel determinations, are untouched.

### Minimum-tax depletion basis

#### United States v. Hill, 506 U.S. 546 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1421.

**Decided:** January 25, 1993.

##### Mineral-interest basis under former §57(a)(8)

**Holding:** For tax years governed by former § 57(a)(8), the adjusted basis offset against percentage depletion is the adjusted basis of the taxpayer’s § 614 interest in the mineral deposit, determined without that year’s depletion deduction; it excludes separately depreciable tangible drilling and development improvements identified in Treasury Regulation § 1.612-4(c)(1). Lawfully capitalized intangible costs recoverable through depletion remain in depletable basis; this classification does not erase distinct statutory provisions governing other costs or later taxable years.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s opinion for the Court, joined in full by the other eight Justices. All nine Justices adopt this rule and its explanation; it controls by a direct majority.

**Current force:** Remains controlling for liabilities governed by former §57(a)(8), including the 1981–1982 years adjudicated. The Energy Policy Act of 1992, Pub. L. 102-486 §1915(a), (d), enacted October 24, 1992, removes §613A(c) depletion deductions from amended §57(a)(1) for taxable years beginning after December 31, 1992. Its coordinated §56(g)(4)(F)(ii) exception likewise removes those deductions from clause (i) and subparagraph (C)(i) for those taxable years; clause (i)’s general cost-depletion rule retains its separate condition for property placed in service in a taxable year beginning after December 31, 1989. The holding does not supply a contrary rule for the amended years.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Energy Policy Act of 1992, Pub. L. 102-486 §1915(a), (d), 106 Stat. 3023–3024; enacted October 24, 1992; applicable to taxable years beginning after December 31, 1992; prospective depletion-preference and adjusted-current-earnings exceptions. This is noncase-law scope information, not a judicial holding.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- INDOPCO, Inc. v. Commissioner, February 26, 1992, left undisturbed and distinguished by issue: capitalization can be required without creating a separate asset; Hill concerns allocation among already distinct depreciation and depletion accounts. No restrictive separate-asset prerequisite is adopted.
The controlling classification follows the coordinated statutory and regulatory text. No Supreme Court precedent is overruled or made into a general rule against multiple tax benefits. Technical Advice Memorandum 8314011 supplies no precedent under then-§ 6110(j)(3); its § 616 deferred-development setting also has an express basis direction different from the costs here. The memorandum is not a judicial holding.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The former-law classification does not combine depreciation and depletion accounts because assets are commercially integrated. Properly capitalized depletable intangible costs remain in mineral basis; no general ban on multiple tax benefits is adopted. Technical Advice Memorandum 8314011 is nonprecedential and its §616 provision has a distinct express basis direction.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the Federal Circuit and remand for implementation of the mineral-interest basis calculation for the 1981–1982 liabilities. The prospective 1992 amendment does not govern those years; no new factual hearing is directed.

### Priority of federal tax liens

#### United States v. McDermott, 507 U.S. 447 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1229.

**Decided:** March 24, 1993.

##### Federal notice precedes choateness in after-acquired property

**Holding:** A filed federal tax lien takes priority over a previously docketed judgment lien in land acquired only after federal notice, because the judgment lien becomes choate in that land only upon acquisition. Both liens attach when the debtor acquires the property; priority here follows the federal notice rule, not fictional earlier attachment.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court, I; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter (6 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- New Britain, 347 U.S. 81 — applied: choateness requires established lienor, amount and property.
- Glass City Bank, 326 U.S. 265 — applied: federal liens reach after-acquired property upon acquisition.
- Vermont, 377 U.S. 351 — distinguished: no separate levy is required for a lien already fixed in existing property; future acquisition remains different.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The priority concerns the September 23 land and substituted proceeds; statutory protections for other creditors and financing interests remain. An asserted earlier contract interest was not preserved within this dispute and is not adjudicated.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the Tenth Circuit and implement federal-lien priority in the escrowed proceeds; no unrelated lien or property is adjudicated.

### Pension property transfers and prohibited exchanges

#### Commissioner v. Keystone Consolidated Industries, Inc., 508 U.S. 152 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1677.
**Decided:** May 24, 1993.

##### The established tax meaning includes property exchanged for release of a funding duty

**Holding:** In §4975(c)(1)(A), the established tax meaning of sale or exchange includes an employer’s transfer of property in satisfaction of an enforceable pension-funding obligation. An unencumbered transfer meeting that condition is not excluded merely because it is called a contribution.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s Part III-A; White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Helvering v. Hammel, 311 U.S.504: settled tax treatment of obligation-discharging transfers applied.
- Sorenson v. Secretary of Treasury, 475 U.S.851: consistent tax-language construction applied.
- Atlantic Cleaners & Dyers, Inc. v. United States, 286 U.S.427: contextual qualification preserved; it does not justify a different meaning here.
- Commissioner v. Lester, 366 U.S.299: internal consistency of tax language supports the established-meaning rationale.
- Albernaz v. United States, 450 U.S.333: prior settled construction informs the meaning Congress selected.

##### Discharge supplies consideration even without importing a technical tax meaning

**Independent alternative holding:** Independently of a settled technical tax meaning, transferring property to discharge an employer’s pension-funding duty is an exchange under §4975(c)(1)(A): the plan receives property and the employer receives release from its obligation. Section4975(f)(3)’s inclusion of property subject to a mortgage or similar lien assumed by the plan or placed by a disqualified person within ten years does not exclude other exchanges supported by consideration.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** Blackmun’s Parts III-C and III-D; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The settled-tax-meaning and ordinary-exchange grounds are independently sufficient. Gratuitous contributions, unpresented exemptions, correction conditions, valuation and computation remain distinct; no agency-deference holding is necessary.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand under the correct transfer classification and applicable tax conditions; unencumbered status alone supplies no exclusion. No automatic second-tier assessment or final tax computation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- This independently sufficient ground rests directly on §4975(c)(1)(A) and (f)(3); it requires no additional treatment of an earlier decision.

## Holdings volume: federal-indian-law

# SUPREME COURT OF THE UNITED STATES: HOLDINGS REGISTER

**Last completed October Term:** 1992  
**Processed through:** July 26, 1993, after DeBoer v. DeBoer, No. A-64.  
**Edition:** September 17, 2026

## Federal Indian Law

### State taxation of reservation fee land

#### County of Yakima v. Confederated Tribes and Bands of the Yakima Indian Nation, 502 U.S. 251 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 90-408 and 90-577.
**Decided:** January 14, 1992.

##### Whether state ad valorem taxation may reach reservation land patented in fee under the General Allotment Act

**Holding:** The General Allotment Act's established fee-land rule and the Burke Act proviso authorize state ad valorem taxation of reservation land validly patented in fee under that Act, including qualifying land later held by the Nation. The tax follows the land, but patent source and independently applicable federal protection must be determined parcel by parcel.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion, joined by Stone, White, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, and Thomas; these eight Justices support the ad valorem holding. Blackmun dissents on this component.

**Material application:** The stipulated record did not establish every parcel's patent source. The Indian Reorganization Act did not automatically restore every existing fee parcel to tax-protected status, and the specific land-tax authority did not create general personal taxing jurisdiction over reservation Indians.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The holding concerns qualifying General Allotment Act fee land and preserves independently applicable federal protections. It does not rest on alienability alone or authorize a governmental-impact inquiry under Brendale.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The judgment is affirmed and the case remanded with modified instructions for parcel-specific patent-source analysis and relief consistent with the two distinct tax holdings.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Goudy is reaffirmed for taxation of qualifying General Allotment Act fee land. Blackfeet supplies the requirement of unmistakably clear congressional authorization. Mancari requires compatible enactments to operate together. Moe's rejection of general personal taxing jurisdiction is preserved. Brendale's fractured analysis of tribal regulation of nonmember land supplies no governmental-impact test for this statutory tax question.

##### Whether permission to tax the land also authorizes a sales excise imposed on an Indian owner

**Holding:** Congressional permission to tax qualifying fee land ad valorem does not unmistakably authorize a tax imposed on an Indian owner's sale of that land. Securing the transactional tax with a lien does not change its legal incidence.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion states this proposition for all nine Justices; all nine Justices support the sales-excise holding.

**Material application:** The annual property levy is triggered by ownership and burdens the realty; the sales excise is triggered by a transaction. A collection lien cannot enlarge Congress's permission by transforming an unauthorized transactional charge into a land tax.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The sales excise remains prohibited, and remand relief must respect that prohibition.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Blackfeet requires authorization clear at the level of the tax actually imposed. Capoeman's allotment-context discussion of taxation after fee patent does not supply blanket permission for every tax related to land.

### Kansas criminal jurisdiction

#### Negonsott v. Samuels, 507 U.S. 99 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-5397.

**Decided:** February 24, 1993.

##### Kansas-specific concurrent authority over state offenses

**Holding:** Under 18 U.S.C. §3243, Kansas may prosecute offenses committed by or against Indians on Indian reservations, including trust or restricted allotments, within Kansas to the same extent its courts may prosecute offenses elsewhere in the State under state law, even when the conduct also violates the Indian Major Crimes Act. Federal courts retain their jurisdiction over federally defined offenses; this concurrent authority over conduct does not authorize Kansas courts to adjudicate federal criminal charges.

**Proposition-level authority:** White's unanimous opinion, joined by the other eight Justices, controls the grant, the saving clause and the disposition.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- County of Yakima v. Confederated Tribes and Bands of the Yakima Indian Nation (January 14, 1992): its requirement of specific congressional authorization is applied at that level; its land-tax holding is neither converted into criminal jurisdiction nor enlarged.
- No earlier decision is overruled. The ordinary federal allocation under the Indian Major Crimes Act remains subject to this express Kansas-specific enactment.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Authority rests on this express Kansas enactment, not general state police power. Other States, tribal adjudicatory authority, double jeopardy and successive prosecutions are not decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm denial of relief from Kansas’s aggravated-battery conviction; the state prosecution falls within §3243.

### State taxation of resident tribal members

#### Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Sac and Fox Nation, 508 U.S. 114 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-259.
**Decided:** May 17, 1993.

##### Member income protection depends on Indian-country residence and source

**Holding:** Absent express congressional authorization, a State may not tax a tribal member’s income earned in Indian country when the member resides there; tribal employment alone does not establish that protection. The residence inquiry includes §1151 reservations, dependent Indian communities and allotments whose Indian titles have not been extinguished, including rights-of-way, rather than only an undiminished formal reservation.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- McClanahan v. Arizona State Tax Commission, 411 U.S.164: applied with both residence and income-source predicates.
- Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Citizen Band Potawatomi Indian Tribe, 498 U.S.505: applied against a reservation-label-only geography.
- County of Yakima v. Confederated Tribes, January 14, 1992: distinguished; limited land-tax authorization supplies no income-tax authority.

##### The challenged vehicle levies cannot be sustained merely as off-country use charges

**Holding:** For tribal members residing in Indian country, the challenged vehicle excise and registration levies fall within the prohibition on state property taxation absent congressional authorization; collecting the same forbidden charges from later purchasers does not save them. Their application to the established residents must be reconsidered on remand; the Court does not decide a genuinely apportioned off-country road-use charge or every tax connected to a vehicle sale.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Tribal employment or registration alone does not establish protection. Outside-Indian-country self-government claims, genuinely apportioned off-country road-use charges and nonmember taxation are not decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate in the reviewed respects and remand for actual member residence, income source and the vehicle levies. Qualifying allotments or other Indian country may resolve residence without deciding every reservation boundary; no blanket member exemption or refund amount.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Moe v. Confederated Salish and Kootenai Tribes, 425 U.S.463: applied to member vehicle property taxation.
- Washington v. Confederated Tribes of the Colville Indian Reservation, 447 U.S.134: applied to the substance of the vehicle levies; genuinely tailored off-country use charges remain distinct.

### Treaty exclusion and acquired recreation lands

#### South Dakota v. Bourland, 508 U.S. 679 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-2051.
**Decided:** June 14, 1993.

##### Acquisition displaces exclusion-derived regulation

**Holding:** The Flood Control Act and Cheyenne River Act displaced the treaty right of exclusive occupation on the taken former trust lands and the non-Indian hunting-and-fishing regulation derived from that exclusion right. Retained tribal access and personal hunting-and-fishing privileges do not themselves preserve authority to license or regulate non-Indian public users.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, Acquisition displaces exclusion-derived regulation portion; White, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Dion, 476 U.S.734 — clear congressional choice required, without an invariable express-words requirement.
- Montana, 450 U.S.544 — exclusion-derived authority and effects of alienation applied.
- Brendale, 492 U.S.408 — land-specific setting considered without inventing a universal rule from its fracture.
- Menominee, 391 U.S.404 — protection against casual implied abrogation preserved; particular enactments distinguished.

##### Residual authority remains a distinct inquiry

**Holding:** Loss of treaty-based exclusion does not decide whether a Montana exception or properly established federal authorization permits particular regulation. Remand preserves the exceptions for consensual commercial dealings, contracts, leases or other arrangements with the Tribe or its members, and for conduct threatening or directly affecting tribal political integrity, economic security, health or welfare, within Montana’s limits. General inherent sovereignty alone does not restore the abrogated regulatory power.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, Residual authority remains a distinct inquiry portion; White, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Taken former trust lands and formerly non-Indian fee lands retain distinct histories. Unreviewed Montana findings and properly established federal authorization remain for consideration; the undeveloped Corps litigating position receives no deference. No reservation-boundary, criminal-jurisdiction or universal exclusive-state-power ruling.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand for residual authority and actual superior-federal-rule conflicts without reviving the displaced treaty exclusion. No result on the undeveloped questions is directed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Montana, 450 U.S.544 — both consensual-relationship and serious tribal-interest exceptions expressly retained.
- United States v. Wheeler, 435 U.S.313 — retained internal tribal powers do not themselves establish nonmember regulation after the abrogation found here.

## Holdings volume: federal-courts

# SUPREME COURT OF THE UNITED STATES: HOLDINGS REGISTER

**Last completed October Term:** 1992  
**Processed through:** July 26, 1993, after DeBoer v. DeBoer, No. A-64.  
**Edition:** September 17, 2026

## Federal Courts

### Adequate and independent state grounds

#### Sochor v. Florida, 504 U.S. 527 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-5843.
**Decided:** June 8, 1992.

For the judicial aggravator, general advisory recommendation and sentence-correction holdings in *Sochor v. Florida*, June 8, 1992, No. 91-5843, see Criminal Procedure — Capital sentencing.

##### Whether Florida's express failure-to-object ground forecloses review of the jury-heinousness instruction despite alternative merits discussion

**Holding:** The Court cannot review a federal challenge to a penalty instruction when the state judgment clearly rests on an adequate independent failure-to-object ground, even if the state court also rejects the claim on its merits. Florida's express preservation ruling forecloses this jury-heinousness challenge; no sufficient basis to reject its adequacy or independence is established.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II-A of Souter's opinion, joined by Stone, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas; seven Justices. Blackmun and Stevens would reach the instructional claim.

**Material application:** Florida separately stated that the penalty-instruction claims were unpreserved and rejected their merits; its note includes heinousness. Availability of fundamental-error review does not establish that this preservation ruling depends on the federal merits, and later citations to the merits discussion do not withdraw the independent ground.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not decide the instruction's constitutional merits or foreclose a supported challenge to a different procedural ground. This direct-review holding creates no habeas exception.

**Operative remedy or transition:** No relief is granted on the jury-heinousness instruction claim; the separate judicial weighing error and inadequate cure require the sentence vacatur described under Criminal Procedure — Capital sentencing.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Michigan v. Long*, 463 U.S. 1032, preserves an expressly independent state disposition despite alternative federal merits discussion. *Herb v. Pitcairn*, 324 U.S. 117, supplies the jurisdictional limit. *Coleman v. Thompson*, 501 U.S. 722, retains its distinction between independent state procedure and federal merits.

### Administrative exhaustion

#### McCarthy v. Madigan, 503 U.S. 140 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-6861.
**Decided:** March 4, 1992.

##### Whether Congress requires exhaustion of the general BOP grievance procedure for this federal medical-care damages action

**Holding:** An express congressional exhaustion requirement controls according to its terms. Where Congress has not required exhaustion, courts must exercise sound discretion consistent with the statutory scheme, weighing prompt access to court against the actual interests in agency authority and judicial efficiency; neither the general BOP delegation nor the then-current §1997e imposes a mandatory gate for this federal prisoner’s *Bivens* medical-care claim.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s opinion, joined by Stone, White, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter; seven Justices control the governing inquiry and statutory application.

**Current force:** Remains controlling as stated under the statutory scheme adjudicated.

**Material application:** General prison-administration authority under 18 U.S.C. §4042 contains no exhaustion command. The then-current 42 U.S.C. §1997e addresses specified state-prisoner §1983 actions through limited discretionary stays, not this federal action. The Bureau’s general grievance procedure is not a comprehensive congressional replacement for the existing medical-care damages channel.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No exception to an express congressional requirement or new *Bivens* action is created. Liability, immunity, causation and damages are not determined.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Patsy v. Board of Regents of Florida*, 457 U.S. 496, makes congressional design central. *McKart v. United States*, 395 U.S. 185, supplies the agency-authority, expertise, error-correction and efficiency purposes. *McGee v. United States*, 402 U.S. 479, supports sound judicial discretion absent congressional mandate. *Coit Independence Joint Venture v. FSLIC*, 489 U.S. 561, distinguishes statutory exhaustion and the adequacy of the administrative route. *Carlson v. Green*, 446 U.S. 14, remains the established medical-care damages channel without extension. *Bush v. Lucas*, 462 U.S. 367, and *Schweiker v. Chilicky*, 487 U.S. 412, are distinguished because there is no comparable comprehensive congressional remedial replacement here.

##### Whether the actual BOP procedure justifies requiring exhaustion of this damages-only claim absent a statutory mandate

**Holding:** Absent a statutory mandate, a federal prisoner seeking only damages for the completed medical-care wrong alleged here need not exhaust the described BOP process: it cannot award compensation, its short filing periods risk forfeiture, and its limited adjudicative benefit does not outweigh access to court. The result does not decide a request for prospective relief, a procedure able to grant the sought compensation, or a materially different administrative adjudication.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s opinion, joined by Stone, White, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter; seven Justices join this particular balancing and application. Scalia and Thomas concur in the judgment solely because monetary relief is unavailable; their votes do not support the deadline or broader balancing rationale.

**Current force:** Remains controlling as stated for this claim and grievance procedure.

**Material application:** The process provides investigation and written responses but no hearing, formal evidentiary findings or monetary award. It requires an initial complaint within 15 days and appeals within 20 and 30 days, with extensions for valid reasons. The rejected regional appeal illustrates forfeiture risk without establishing official misconduct. Speculative FTCA conversion or settlement does not establish an effective damages remedy under these regulations.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The deadline analysis does not invalidate short filing periods categorically. No finding of bias, deliberate indifference, damages entitlement or loss of a defense is made. The holding does not convert negligence into a constitutional violation or expand the established damages action.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the exhaustion dismissal and remand for ordinary proceedings on the damages complaint without completion of this grievance process as a prerequisite. Liability, defenses and damages remain for adjudication.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *McKart*, 395 U.S. 185, supports comparing actual individual and institutional interests. *Coit*, 489 U.S. 561, informs practical adequacy without categorically invalidating deadlines. *Turner v. Safley*, 482 U.S. 78, preserves judicial cognizance of valid prisoner claims without deciding this claim’s validity. *Hillsborough Township v. Cromwell*, 326 U.S. 620, and *Union Pacific Railroad Co. v. Board of County Commissioners*, 247 U.S. 282, address uncertainty in an administrative route to the requested relief.

### Appellate jurisdiction and notices of appeal

#### Smith v. Barry, 502 U.S. 244 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-7477.
**Decided:** January 14, 1992.

##### Whether a timely appellate brief may function as a notice of appeal

**Holding:** A timely document styled as an appellate brief may serve as a notice of appeal if it objectively identifies the appealing party, the judgment or order appealed, and the appellate court as Rule 3(c) requires. Rule 4 treats a notice mistakenly filed in the court of appeals as filed in the district court upon receipt; informal form or title alone cannot require dismissal.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's opinion, joined by Stone, White, Blackmun, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, and Thomas; these eight Justices supply the controlling rationale. Scalia concurs in the judgment and agrees with the express form-or-title ground.

**Material application:** Smith returned the clerk's informal-brief form within the valid appeal period. Use of that form did not negate the notice the document objectively might convey, and a requirement of separate notice and brief documents could not add a jurisdictional condition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Each item required by Rule 3 remains necessary. The first notice remains ineffective under the then-current posttrial-motion rule. The Court does not decide whether the May 4 document actually supplies each required item or which judgment or orders it fairly identifies.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The judgment is reversed and the case remanded for the Fourth Circuit to examine the informal brief's actual contents.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Torres makes Rule 3 compliance mandatory while allowing functional compliance with its required information. Foman supports contextual reading where intent to seek review is manifest. Griggs is applied. Ibarra remains confined to timely criminal reconsideration motions.

### Appellate stays and mandatory agency relief

#### Benten v. Kessler, 505 U.S. 1084 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** Application No. A-40.
**Decided:** July 17, 1992.

##### A substantial procedural challenge does not alone establish entitlement to mandatory return

**Holding:** An applicant seeking to vacate an appellate stay of an order granting effectively final individual relief must show a substantial likelihood of entitlement to that ordered relief and a demonstrable error in the appellate stay. Where the asserted procedural defect would restore agency discretion rather than compel the requested substantive outcome, a strong procedural claim and serious irreparable injury do not alone establish entitlement to immediate judicially ordered return of the drug.

**Proposition-level authority:** The per curiam opinion is joined by Stone-Zsela, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas: seven Justices. The Court assumes the procedural challenge's strength for this remedial inquiry; it does not adopt Stone-Zsela's separate assessment that the then-operative FDA procedural regulation was likely violated.

**Material application:** The personal-use policy permits admission rather than compels it. Even assuming a strong procedural challenge, removing automatic detention restores agency discretion. The Court accepts serious imminent injury without inventing individual danger or an alternative treatment placement.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not finally uphold the Import Alert, reject the asserted 1989 procedural undertaking, establish an across-the-board exhaustion command, decide §10.70 or arbitrary-or-capricious merits, or deny the possibility of prompt lawful agency-action relief. The constitutional claim discussed by Stevens was not addressed below or presented in the applicants' filings here and is not properly before the Court. Neither that claim nor the right to a particular abortion method is decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The application to vacate the Second Circuit's July 15 stay is denied, 7–2. The appellate stay retains its terms; the District Court's mandatory return order is not reinstated. No drug approval, general import authorization or constitutional access judgment is entered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Western Airlines, Inc. v. Teamsters, 480 U.S. 1301 (1987) (in chambers): applied for the demanding standard governing intervention in an appellate stay; urgent injury does not eliminate the required merits-and-remedy showing.
- Ford Motor Co. v. NLRB, 305 U.S. 364 (1939): its flexibility to make lawful administrative action effective is preserved; it does not clearly establish entitlement to judicial selection of the discretionary admission decision on this application.
- United States v. Rutherford, 442 U.S. 544 (1979): the statutory approval framework remains in force; this application creates no judicial drug-approval exemption and makes no general safety finding.

### Bankruptcy appellate jurisdiction

#### Connecticut National Bank v. Germain, 503 U.S. 249 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1791.
**Decided:** March 9, 1992.

##### Whether §158(d) precludes otherwise available §1292 review of district-court bankruptcy appellate orders

**Holding:** Section 158(d) does not preclude court-of-appeals review under §1292 of a district court’s order entered in its bankruptcy appellate capacity. Review remains subject to the particular §1292 route’s requirements, including certification and circuit permission where §1292(b) applies; the provision does not grant an automatic appeal.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s opinion, joined by Stone, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; five Justices adopt the text/coexistence rationale. White, Blackmun, Stevens and O’Connor agree in judgment on their separate stated reasoning.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The applicable §1292 requirements remain, including certification of a controlling legal question, substantial ground for difference of opinion, material advancement of termination, timely application and circuit permission under §1292(b). No jury entitlement or extension of district-court language to bankruptcy appellate panels is decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The jurisdictional dismissal is reversed and remanded to the Second Circuit to address the remaining statutory requirements and whether to permit the appeal.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Wood v. United States, 16 Pet. 342 — applied: overlapping statutes remain effective absent positive conflict.
- United States v. Ron Pair Enterprises, 489 U.S. 235 — applied: the enacted bankruptcy-related text governs.
- Rubin v. United States, 449 U.S. 424 — applied: unambiguous statutory words control the inquiry.
- United States v. Goldenberg, 168 U.S. 95 — applied: courts give effect to legislative text rather than assumed omissions.

### Civil jury right

#### Wooddell v. International Brotherhood of Electrical Workers, Local 71, 502 U.S. 93 (1991)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-967.  
**Decided:** December 4, 1991.

##### Whether lost hiring-hall referrals support legal damages carrying a Seventh Amendment jury right

**Holding:** A union member's claim for wages and benefits lost because hiring-hall referrals allegedly were withheld is a claim for compensatory legal relief carrying a Seventh Amendment jury right. Accompanying equitable requests do not eliminate the jury right on the legal issues.

**Proposition-level authority:** White's opinion joined by Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Souter; all eight participants.

**Material application:** Wooddell seeks replacement of lost earnings, not return of identified funds held by the union or backpay incidental to reinstatement in a former job.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Jury entitlement decides the factfinder, not retaliation, liability, or recoverable amount. Equitable relief remains separately adjudicated, with legal and equitable issues sequenced consistently with the Seventh Amendment.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The jury-denial component is reversed and remanded to permit a jury on the LMRDA legal damages claim.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Chauffeurs, Teamsters & Helpers Local 391 v. Terry supplies the historical-analogue inquiry and makes the legal or equitable character of relief the more important consideration.

For the member-beneficiary enforcement holding in Wooddell v. International Brotherhood of Electrical Workers, Local 71, decided December 4, 1991, No. 90-967, see Labor and Employment — Union-constitution enforcement.

### Collateral procedural authority

#### Willy v. Coastal Corp., 503 U.S. 131 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1150.
**Decided:** March 3, 1992.

##### Whether the Rules and Article III permit this Rule 11 sanction after the court is held to lack jurisdiction over the action

**Holding:** A federal district court may impose and preserve a valid Rule 11 sanction for litigation misconduct before it even when its assertion of subject-matter jurisdiction over the action is later held erroneous. The sanction must address an independently authorized procedural violation collateral to the merits; it does not validate the jurisdictional error or authorize adjudication of the underlying claim.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; unanimous holding on both authorization under the Rules and the Article III objection.

**Current force:** Remains controlling as stated for the then-effective Rule 11 and the independent procedural violation adjudicated.

**Material application:** Rules 1 and 81(c) govern civil proceedings after removal, and Rule 11 supplies the particular sanctioning authority. The completed misconduct was misuse of the court’s procedure, not Willy’s successful jurisdictional objection. The propriety of the violation, fee response and joint liability was accepted on this limited review.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No hypothetical merits jurisdiction, general inherent sanctioning power or universal collateral-order exception is recognized. A different order requires its own lawful source and genuine independence. The accepted misconduct and amount are not reconsidered.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the $19,307 sanction, jointly and severally against Willy and his attorney, without enlargement. The prior jurisdictional direction returning the employment claims to state court remains operative; the invalid federal merits judgment is not revived.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Sibbach v. Wilson & Co.*, 312 U.S. 1, preserves the prohibition on procedural rules enlarging substantive rights or jurisdiction. *Cooter & Gell v. Hartmarx Corp.*, 496 U.S. 384, is extended from voluntary dismissal to this erroneous assertion of merits jurisdiction because Rule 11 adjudication is collateral. *United States v. Mine Workers*, 330 U.S. 258, supports orderly determination of disputed authority without assumed merits power. *United States Catholic Conference v. Abortion Rights Mobilization, Inc.*, 487 U.S. 72, is distinguished: coercive discovery serving unauthorized merits adjudication lacks the independence of this completed sanction.

### Diversity jurisdiction and domestic-relations abstention

#### Ankenbrandt v. Richards, 504 U.S. 689 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-367.
**Decided:** June 15, 1992.

##### Whether Article III or the domestic-relations exception to §1332 excludes this diverse tort damages action

**Holding:** Article III does not exclude domestic-relations disputes from the federal judicial power. Section 1332 carries a settled domestic-relations exception limited to issuance of divorce, alimony, and child-custody decrees; that exception does not exclude a diverse parties’ tort action seeking damages for alleged child abuse without such a decree.

**Proposition-level authority:** White's opinion of the Court, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; five Justices adopt this proposition. The judgment reversing dismissal is unanimous, but the separate judgment concurrences do not add joins to the Court's foundational statutory holding.

**Material application:** The complaint seeks compensation from the children's father and his companion for alleged abuse, without requesting a divorce, alimony, custody decree or review of a state decree. The settled statutory understanding survives the 1948 recodification; Article III itself contains no domestic-relations exclusion.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The exception concerns issuance of divorce, alimony and child-custody decrees, not every dispute among relatives. No federal tort is created; liability, parental immunity, damages, the alleged abuse and any custody or support decree are not adjudicated.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Fifth Circuit judgment is reversed and the complaint remanded for ordinary proceedings and defenses. The Court issues or modifies no domestic decree and awards no damages.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Barber v. Barber, 62 U.S. (21 How.) 582 (1859): retained as the basis for the limited decree exception and distinguished from a prohibition of ordinary damages actions.
- De la Rama v. De la Rama, 201 U.S. 303 (1906): its distinction between territorial authority and ordinary decree jurisdiction remains; it does not exclude this tort action.
- In re Burrus, 136 U.S. 586 (1890): broad domestic-relations language does not enlarge the exception to this diverse damages suit.
- Patterson v. McLean Credit Union, 491 U.S. 164 (1989): statutory stare decisis supports preservation of the settled limited construction; no separate civil-rights issue is decided.
- Fourco Glass Co. v. Transmirra Products Corp., 353 U.S. 222 (1957): a recodification is not presumed to alter substantive law absent a clear indication.
- Lorillard v. Pons, 434 U.S. 575 (1978): congressional retention of language against an established judicial construction supports the retained limited exception.

##### Whether Younger abstention supports dismissal without an ongoing state proceeding

**Holding:** Younger abstention cannot sustain this dismissal because there is no ongoing state proceeding with which the federal damages action would interfere.

**Proposition-level authority:** White's opinion of the Court, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; five Justices adopt this proposition. The judgment reversing dismissal is unanimous, but the separate judgment concurrences do not add joins to the Court's foundational statutory holding.

**Material application:** The action seeks compensation for past misconduct and does not halt, supervise or displace an ongoing state adjudication. No state proceeding is alleged to be pending.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court leaves Younger's outer range in other domestic-relations controversies undecided. A family setting or the possibility of a future state application supplies no ongoing proceeding.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Younger cannot sustain the dismissal; ordinary proceedings on the damages complaint must resume, subject to the separately stated limits.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Younger v. Harris, 401 U.S. 37 (1971): retained; its ongoing-proceeding premise is absent here.
- New Orleans Public Service, Inc. v. Council of New Orleans, 491 U.S. 350 (1989): applied to require a concrete abstention basis rather than general reluctance to exercise federal jurisdiction.

##### Whether Burford abstention supports dismissal without unsettled domestic status or relevant state-policy interference

**Holding:** Burford abstention does not authorize dismissal of this ordinary diversity tort action where adjudication neither requires resolution of an unsettled domestic status nor threatens the state-policy interference that supports that form of abstention.

**Proposition-level authority:** White's opinion of the Court, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; five Justices adopt this proposition. The judgment reversing dismissal is unanimous, but the separate judgment concurrences do not add joins to the Court's foundational statutory holding.

**Material application:** Family status has already been determined and its present legal character does not decide the alleged torts or compensation. State expertise and an important family-policy interest alone cannot replace the absent interference.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Materially different future circumstances remain open. An unresolved status issue necessary to the federal action together with threatened interference in the relevant state process would present a different question; that hypothetical is not adjudicated here.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Burford cannot sustain this dismissal; the reinstated tort complaint returns for ordinary proceedings and defenses.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Burford v. Sun Oil Co., 319 U.S. 315 (1943): retained within its state-policy and administrative-coherence rationale; no basis for abstention is shown here.
- New Orleans Public Service, Inc. v. Council of New Orleans, 491 U.S. 350 (1989): applied to confine Burford abstention to concrete interference, not the mere importance of state interests.

### Execution stays

#### Coleman v. Thompson, 504 U.S. 188 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. A-877; associated petition No. 91-8336.
**Decided:** May 20, 1992.

##### Whether the renewed habeas presentation satisfies the requirements for a stay pending certiorari review

**Holding:** A stay of execution pending certiorari review requires a reasonable probability that four Justices would find the issue sufficiently meritorious to grant review, a significant possibility of reversal, and likely irreparable harm without a stay. Coleman's combined presentation does not establish the necessary possibility of overcoming the surviving independent claim-default and successive-petition barriers; denial decides neither ultimate innocence nor a general freestanding-innocence or hearing rule.

**Proposition-level authority:** The per curiam opinion is joined by White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas: five of nine participating Justices adopt this record-specific application of Barefoot. Stevens concurs in the denial without joining that opinion. Stone, Blackmun and Souter dissent.

**Material application:** The disputed recantation, competing confession accounts, physical-evidence allegations and scientific interpretations are considered together but do not show the needed prospect of overcoming independent claim-default and successive-petition barriers. Irreparable harm alone does not establish the missing likelihood.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court decides no ultimate innocence, freestanding-innocence merits rule, conclusive-exoneration requirement, automatic hearing entitlement, categorical credibility or expert-selection rule, or reversal of the preterm Coleman holding.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The stay application is denied, 6–3. The denial is an interim disposition and does not finally decide associated petition No. 91-8336.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Barefoot v. Estelle, 463 U.S. 880 (1983): applied; the ordinary capital-stay prerequisites remain distinct and no automatic stay is created.
- Coleman v. Thompson, 501 U.S. 722 (1991): applied; independent and adequate state claim-default rules and their established exceptions remain in force.
- McCleskey v. Zant, 499 U.S. 467 (1991): applied to the independent successive-petition and abuse barriers; its cause-and-prejudice rule and innocence exception are not replaced.
- Murray v. Carrier, 477 U.S. 478 (1986): applied for the extraordinary miscarriage-of-justice exception; the Court does not require conclusive exoneration.
